Symplectic Structures on the Space of Space Curves
Authors: Martin Bauer, Sadashige Ishida, Peter W. Michor
Published: 2026

ABSTRACT
We present symplectic structures on the shape space of unparameterized space curves that generalize the classical Marsden–Weinstein structure. Our method integrates the Liouville 1-form of the Marsden–Weinstein structure with Riemannian structures that have been introduced in mathematical shape analysis. We also derive Hamiltonian vector fields for several classical Hamiltonian functions with respect to these new symplectic structures.Supplementary InformationThe online version contains supplementary material available at 10.1007/s00332-026-10266-8.

Introduction

Motivation and Background

The space of unparametrized space curves\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {B_i(S^1,{\mathbb {R}}^3):=\textrm{Imm}(S^1,\mathbb {R}^3)/\operatorname {Diff}(S^1)} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Bi(S1,R3):=Imm(S1,R3)/Diff(S1)as an infinite-dimensional orbifold is known to have a symplectic structure called the Marsden–Weinstein structure (MW structure) (Marsden and Weinstein 1983). It is thought of as a canonical symplectic structure as it is formally a Kirillov-Kostant-Souriau form by regarding space curves as linear functionals on the space of divergence-free vector fields in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathbb {R}}^3$$\end{document}R3; see, e.g., (Marsden and Weinstein 1983, Theorem 4.2) and (Arnold and Khesin 2021, Chapter VI, Proposition 3.6). Another incentive for studying the MW symplectic structure can be found in its appearance in mathematical fluid dynamics: For example, one can interpret vortex filaments as the MW flow of the kinetic energy of the velocity field induced by vorticity concentrated on the curve.

Via so-called localized induction approximation vortex filaments reduce to the binormal flow, which is a completely integrable system and is again an MW flow for the length functional as the Hamiltonian; see, e.g., (Saffman 1993, Chapter 11) or (Majda and Bertozzi 2001, Chapter 7) and the references therein.

To the best of the authors’ knowledge, to date no symplectic structures other than the MW form have been studied on the space of unparametrized space curves. Riemannian structures on this space, on the other hand, have attracted a significant amount of interest; primarily due to their relevance to mathematical shape analysis (Younes 2010; Srivastava and Klassen 2016; Bauer et al. 2014). The arguably most natural such metric, the reparametrization-invariant \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^2$$\end{document}L2-metric admits a surprising degeneracy: the geodesic distance between any pair of curves vanishes on both the space of parametrized and unparametrized curves (Michor and Mumford 2005; Bauer et al. 2012). This result renders the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^2$$\end{document}L2-metric unsuited as a basis for mathematical shape analysis and thus started a quest for stronger Riemannian metrics, which induce a non-degenerate distance function and consequently can be used for applications in these areas (see, e.g., Michor and Mumford (2006); Yezzi and Mennucci (2005); Srivastava et al. (2010); Bauer et al. (2017) and the references therein).

The aforementioned \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^2$$\end{document}L2-Riemannian metric and the MW symplectic structure are related via an almost complex structure, which is induced on shape space by the cross-product with the unit tangent vector of the curve c, i.e., \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J_c(h):=\frac{c_\theta }{|c_\theta |} \times h$$\end{document}Jc(h):=cθ|cθ|×h; here \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c:S^1\rightarrow \mathbb {R}^3$$\end{document}c:S1→R3 is a space curve and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h:S^1\rightarrow \mathbb {R}^3$$\end{document}h:S1→R3 is a tangent vector to c. Furthermore, the MW symplectic structure \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$B_i(S^1,{\mathbb {R}}^3)$$\end{document}Bi(S1,R3).

Main Contributions

These relations between Riemannian geometry and symplectic geometry on the space of space curves are the starting point of the present article: our principal goal is to construct new symplectic structures on the space of unparametrized curves by combining the above classical construction with more recent advances in Riemannian geometry of these spaces, i.e., we construct new presymplectic structures by modifying the Liouville form of the MW form using different Riemannian metrics from mathematical shape analysis. This construction automatically leads to a closed 2-form (and thus a presymplectic form) on the space of parametrized curves. Under certain assumptions on the Riemannian metric this form then descends to a presymplectic structure on the space of unparametrized space curves, and thus, it only remains to check the non-degeneracy of this 2-form to conclude that it is (weakly) symplectic. Proving this property turns out to be surprisingly difficult and provides the main technical contribution of the present article. Interestingly, in some cases the presymplectic form still has a non-trivial kernel on the shape space, but becomes symplectic when the quotient by a further two-dimensional foliation is taken.

We also derive formulae for Hamiltonian vector fields of several classical Hamiltonian functions generated by our new symplectic structures and provide numerical illustrations to qualitatively show a few simple examples among these new Hamiltonian flows. The Riemannian counterparts can be found in the area of geometric gradient flows on the space of curves, where the investigations of gradient flows for certain well-known energy functionals (e.g., the entropy or length energy functional) for Riemannian metrics other than the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^2$$\end{document}L2-Riemannian metric has been recently initiated (see, e.g., the work of Okabe, Schrader, Wheeler and Wheeler (Okabe et al. 2023; Schrader et al. 2023). In our investigations, we observe that, for certain choices of Hamiltonian and symplectic structure, we obtain a new representation of well-known Hamiltonian flows, i.e., we may reproduce Hamiltonian flows of the MW symplectic structure from a different pair of a symplectic structure and a Hamiltonian function. For other examples, we obtain genuinely new Hamiltonian flows, which do not seem to be represented as a Hamiltonian flow for the MW symplectic structure.

A seemingly more straightforward approach to obtain new symplectic structures can be found in directly defining a new skew symmetric 2-form via alternating the Riemannian metric and combining it with the almost complex structure \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {J}$$\end{document}J from the MW symplectic structure. This approach turns out to be somewhat unsuccessful as the resulting skew symmetric 2-form is usually not closed and thus not even presymplectic. We discuss this approach and the resulting 2-forms in Appendix A. This further highlights the non-trivial challenge of finding a 2-form which is both closed and non-degenerate, rather than one being merely closed or non-degenerate. This observation was our original incentive to follow the slightly more complicated procedure described above. Finally, in Appendix B, we provide a short introduction to infinite-dimensional weak symplectic geometry, including a new assumption that was overlooked in previous research.

Future Directions

In this article, we introduced new (pre)symplectic structures on the shape space of space curves. Our procedure of modifying the Liouville form of a (pre)symplectic form and taking the exterior derivative is not limited to such shape spaces. It would be interesting to apply the same machinery for other infinite-dimensional (weak-)symplectic manifolds that admit Liouville forms such as the space of complex functions on a domain or the cotangent bundle of an infinite-dimensional Riemannian manifold.

At this point, the connection between new Hamiltonian flows and existing physical theories seems to remain unclear. Hence we are also keen to use this new framework to find new interpretations of physically relevant quantities as Hamiltonian flows, in a similar way that certain compressible fluids are modeled as geodesic flows of higher-order metrics (Mumford and Michor 2013).

Structure of the Article

In Sect. 2, we introduce Liouville forms via the modification of the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^2$$\end{document}L2-Riemannian metric, and then compute presymplectic forms by taking the exterior derivative. In Sect. 3, we show that, a class of presymplectic structures attained by conformal factors on the shape space are indeed weekly symplectic. We also derive Hamiltonian vector fields with respect to these weak symplectic structures. In Sect. 4, we describe more concretely symplectic structures induced by the length function as a special case of conformal factors and provide several examples of Hamiltonian vector fields. In Sect. 5, we discuss the presymplectic structure induced by the curvature-weighted metric, where we leave the non-degeneracy open for future research. Finally, in Sect. 6, we numerically illustrate simple Hamiltonian flows with respect to symplectic structures induced by length functions.

Liouville Structures and (Pre)symplectic Structures

Remark 2.1

(Sobolev metrics) An important class of such metrics is the class of Sobolev \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H^m$$\end{document}Hm-metrics, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L=(1-(-1)^m D_s^{2m})$$\end{document}L=(1-(-1)mDs2m) with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$D_s=\frac{1}{|c_\theta |}\partial _{\theta }$$\end{document}Ds=1|cθ|∂θ being the arclength derivative. Using the notation \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{d}s=|c_\theta |\textrm{d}\theta $$\end{document}ds=|cθ|dθ for the arclength measure we obtain for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$m=0$$\end{document}m=0 the metric\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} G_c^{\operatorname {id}}(h,k)&= \int _{S^1} \langle h,k\rangle |{c_\theta }| \textrm{d}\theta =\int _{S^1} \langle h,k\rangle \textrm{d}s \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Gcid(h,k)=∫S1⟨h,k⟩|cθ|dθ=∫S1⟨h,k⟩dsand for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$m=1$$\end{document}m=1 the metric\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}&G_c^{\operatorname {id}-D_s^2}(h,k) \\&= \int _{S^1} \langle h,k\rangle +\langle - D_s^2 h, k \rangle \textrm{d}s = \int _{S^1} \langle h,k\rangle +\langle D_s h,D_s k \rangle \textrm{d}s. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Gcid-Ds2(h,k)=∫S1⟨h,k⟩+⟨-Ds2h,k⟩ds=∫S1⟨h,k⟩+⟨Dsh,Dsk⟩ds.All these metrics can be written in terms of arclength derivative \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$D_s=\frac{1}{|c_\theta |}\partial _\theta $$\end{document}Ds=1|cθ|∂θ and arclength integration \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{d}s=|c_\theta |\textrm{d}\theta $$\end{document}ds=|cθ|dθ only. It has been shown that each such metric induces a corresponding metric on the shape space \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$B_i(S^1,{\mathbb {R}}^3)$$\end{document}Bi(S1,R3) such that the projection \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\pi :\textrm{Imm}(S^1,{\mathbb {R}})\rightarrow B_i(S^1,{\mathbb {R}}^3)$$\end{document}π:Imm(S1,R)→Bi(S1,R3) is a Riemannian submersion (Michor and Mumford 2007). In finite dimension, this would follow directly from the invariance of the metric, but in this infinite-dimensional situation one has to show in addition the existence of the horizontal complement (w.r.t. the Riemannian metric). We will see, however, that this particular class of metrics will not be suited for the purpose of the present paper, as the induced symplectic structure will not descend to a symplectic structure on the quotient space.

The Induced Liouville 1-Form

Next we will use the metric \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G^L$$\end{document}GL to define a (Liouville) 1-form on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{Imm}(S^1,\mathbb {R}^3)$$\end{document}Imm(S1,R3). Therefore we consider for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c\in \textrm{Imm}(S^1,\mathbb {R}^3)$$\end{document}c∈Imm(S1,R3) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h\in T_c\textrm{Imm}(S^1,\mathbb {R}^3)$$\end{document}h∈TcImm(S1,R3) the 1-form:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Theta ^L_c(h):=G^L_c(c\times D_s c, h)=\int \langle c\times D_sc, L_c h\rangle \textrm{d}s=\int \operatorname {det}(c, D_sc, L_c h) \textrm{d}s, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ΘcL(h):=GcL(c×Dsc,h)=∫⟨c×Dsc,Lch⟩ds=∫det(c,Dsc,Lch)ds,where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\times $$\end{document}× denotes the vector cross-product on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {R}^3$$\end{document}R3. We have the following result concerning its invariance properties:

Lemma 2.2

(Liouville 1-form) For any inertia operator L, which is equivariant under the right action of the group of all orientation-preserving diffeomorphisms and the left action of the rotation group SO(3), the induced Liouville 1-form \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Theta ^L$$\end{document}ΘL is invariant under the right action of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\operatorname {Diff}^+(S^1)$$\end{document}Diff+(S1) and the left action of SO(3), i.e., for any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c\in \textrm{Imm}$$\end{document}c∈Imm, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h\in T_c\textrm{Imm}$$\end{document}h∈TcImm, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varphi \in \operatorname {Diff}^+(S^1)$$\end{document}φ∈Diff+(S1) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$O\in SO(3)$$\end{document}O∈SO(3) we have\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Theta ^L_{O(c\circ \varphi )}(O(h\circ \varphi ))=\Theta ^L_c(h). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ΘO(c∘φ)L(O(h∘φ))=ΘcL(h).

Proof

We will only show the reparametrization invariance, the invariance under SO(3) is similar but easier. Using the equivariance of both L and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$D_s$$\end{document}Ds we calculate\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Theta ^L_{c\circ \varphi }(h\circ \varphi )=\int \langle c\circ \varphi \times (D_sc)\circ \varphi , (L_ch)\circ \varphi \rangle |c_\theta |\circ \varphi |\varphi '|\; \textrm{d}\theta =\int \langle c\times D_sc, L_c h\rangle \textrm{d}s=\Theta ^L_c(h). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Θc∘φL(h∘φ)=∫⟨c∘φ×(Dsc)∘φ,(Lch)∘φ⟩|cθ|∘φ|φ′|dθ=∫⟨c×Dsc,Lch⟩ds=ΘcL(h).\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square $$\end{document}□

Remark 2.3

If L is equivariant under the left action of not only SO(3) but of the larger group \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$SL(3)=\{M\in GL(3,{\mathbb {R}})\mid \det (M)=1\}$$\end{document}SL(3)={M∈GL(3,R)∣det(M)=1}, then also \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Theta ^L$$\end{document}ΘL is invariant under SL(3). This is the case for the Marsden–Weinstein structure \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L=\textrm{id}$$\end{document}L=id (see Remark 2.5), but in general not for the inertia operators we deal with in this article.

The Induced (Pre)symplectic Form on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{Imm}(S^1,\mathbb {R}^2)$$\end{document}Imm(S1,R2)

Once we have defined the 1-form \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Theta $$\end{document}Θ we can formally consider the induced symplectic form\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Omega ^L_c(h,k):=-\textrm{d}\Theta ^L_c(h,k)=-D_{c,h}\Theta ^L_c(k)+D_{c,k}\Theta ^L_c(h)+\Theta ^L_c([h,k]), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ΩcL(h,k):=-dΘcL(h,k)=-Dc,hΘcL(k)+Dc,kΘcL(h)+ΘcL([h,k]),where d denotes the exterior derivative, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$D_{c,h}$$\end{document}Dc,h denotes the directional derivative at \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c\in \textrm{Imm}(S^1,{\mathbb {R}}^3)$$\end{document}c∈Imm(S1,R3) in the direction h, and when applied to a function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f:\textrm{Imm}(S^1,{\mathbb {R}}^3)\rightarrow {\mathbb {R}}$$\end{document}f:Imm(S1,R3)→R, we have \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$D_{c,h}f=\mathcal {L}_h f (c)$$\end{document}Dc,hf=Lhf(c). The bracket [h, k] is the Lie-bracket in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {X}(\textrm{Imm}(S^1,\mathbb {R}^3))$$\end{document}X(Imm(S1,R3)) given by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$[h,k]=D_{c,h}k-D_{c,k}h$$\end{document}[h,k]=Dc,hk-Dc,kh.

In the following theorem we calculate this 2-form explicitly:

Theorem 2.4

(The (pre)symplectic form \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Omega ^L$$\end{document}ΩL on parametrized curves) Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c\in \textrm{Imm}(S^1,\mathbb {R}^3)$$\end{document}c∈Imm(S1,R3) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h,k\in T_c\textrm{Imm}(S^1,\mathbb {R}^3)$$\end{document}h,k∈TcImm(S1,R3). We have1\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned} \Omega ^L_c(h,k)&=\int \Big (\langle D_s c, L_c h \times k + h\times L_c k\rangle - \langle c, D_s h \times L_c k + L_ch\times D_s k \rangle \\ &\qquad + \langle c\times D_s c, (D_{c,k}L_c)h-(D_{c,h}L_c)k\rangle \Big ) \textrm{d}s \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ΩcL(h,k)=∫(⟨Dsc,Lch×k+h×Lck⟩-⟨c,Dsh×Lck+Lch×Dsk⟩+⟨c×Dsc,(Dc,kLc)h-(Dc,hLc)k⟩)dsFurthermore, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Omega ^L$$\end{document}ΩL is invariant under the right action of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\operatorname {Diff}^+(S^1)$$\end{document}Diff+(S1) and under the left action of SO(3).

Remark 2.5

(Marsden–Weinstein symplectic structure) It is known that for the invariant \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^2$$\end{document}L2-metric, i.e., \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L=\operatorname {id}$$\end{document}L=id, one obtains three times the Marsden–Weinstein (weak)-symplectic structure with this procedure (see Tabachnikov (2017); Padilla et al. (2019), for example), i.e.,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} 3\Omega ^{\operatorname {MW}}_c(h,k):=\Omega ^\textrm{id}_c(h,k)= 3 \int _{S^1}\langle D_sc\times h,k\rangle \textrm{d}s = 3 \int \det (D_sc,h,k)\textrm{d}s. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}3ΩcMW(h,k):=Ωcid(h,k)=3∫S1⟨Dsc×h,k⟩ds=3∫det(Dsc,h,k)ds.Its kernel consists exactly of all vector fields along c which are tangent to c, so by reduction it induces a presymplectic structure on shape space \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{Imm}(S^1,\mathbb {R}^3)/\operatorname {Diff}^+(S^1)$$\end{document}Imm(S1,R3)/Diff+(S1) which is easily seen to be weakly non-degenerate and thus is a symplectic structure there.

Proof of Theorem 2.4

To prove the formula for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Omega ^L$$\end{document}ΩL, we first collect several variational formulas (see, e.g., Michor and Mumford (2006)) for a proof:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \textrm{d}s&= |c_\theta |d\theta , \quad D_{c,h} \textrm{d}s = \frac{\langle h_\theta ,c_\theta \rangle }{|c_\theta |} d\theta = \langle D_s h, D_s c\rangle \textrm{d}s \\ D_s&=\frac{1}{|c_\theta |}\partial _\theta , \quad D_{c,h}D_s = \frac{-\langle h_\theta , c_\theta \rangle }{|c_\theta |^3}\partial _\theta = -\langle D_s h, D_s c\rangle D_s. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ds=|cθ|dθ,Dc,hds=⟨hθ,cθ⟩|cθ|dθ=⟨Dsh,Dsc⟩dsDs=1|cθ|∂θ,Dc,hDs=-⟨hθ,cθ⟩|cθ|3∂θ=-⟨Dsh,Dsc⟩Ds.Since \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{Imm}(S^1, \mathbb {R}^3)$$\end{document}Imm(S1,R3) is open in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C^\infty (S^1,\mathbb {R}^3)$$\end{document}C∞(S1,R3), we can choose globally constant h, k i.e., independent of the location c on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{Imm}(S^1, \mathbb {R}^3)$$\end{document}Imm(S1,R3), namely \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$D_{c,h}(k)=D_{c,k}(h)=0$$\end{document}Dc,h(k)=Dc,k(h)=0 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} Using \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$D_{c,h}(L_c k)=(D_{c,h}L_c)k+L_c (h(k))=(D_{c,h}L_c)k$$\end{document}Dc,h(Lck)=(Dc,hLc)k+Lc(h(k))=(Dc,hLc)k, we compute\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} D_{c,h}\Theta ^L_c(k)&= \int \Big (\det (h, D_s c, L_c k) -\langle D_s h, D_s c\rangle \det (c, D_s c, L_c k) +\det (c, D_s h, L_c k) \\ &\qquad \qquad +\det (c, D_s c, (D_{c,h}L_c)k) +\langle D_s h, D_s c\rangle \det (c, D_s c, L_c k)\Big )\textrm{d}s \\ &= \int \Big (\det (h, D_s c, L_c k)+\det (c, D_s h, L_c k) +\det (c, D_s c, (D_{c,h}L_c)k)\Big )\textrm{d}s. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Dc,hΘcL(k)=∫(det(h,Dsc,Lck)-⟨Dsh,Dsc⟩det(c,Dsc,Lck)+det(c,Dsh,Lck)+det(c,Dsc,(Dc,hLc)k)+⟨Dsh,Dsc⟩det(c,Dsc,Lck))ds=∫(det(h,Dsc,Lck)+det(c,Dsh,Lck)+det(c,Dsc,(Dc,hLc)k))ds.Thus we get for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Omega ^L$$\end{document}ΩL:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned} \Omega ^L_c(h,k)&= -D_{c,h}\Theta ^L_c(k) + D_{c,k}\Theta ^L_c(h) + 0 \\ &= \int \Big (-\det (h, D_s c, L_c k) + \det (k, D_s c, L_c h) -\det (c, D_s h, L_c k) + \det (c, D_s k, L_c h) \\ &\qquad \qquad -\det \big (c, D_s c, (D_{c,h}L_c)k - (D_{c,k}L_c)h\big )\Big )\textrm{d}s \\ &=\int \Big (\langle D_s c, L_c h \times k + h\times L_c k\rangle - \langle c, D_s h \times L_c k - D_s k \times L_c h \rangle \\ &\qquad \qquad - \langle c\times D_s c, (D_{c,h}L_c)k - The invariance properties of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Omega ^L$$\end{document}ΩL follow directly from the corresponding invariance properties of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Theta ^L$$\end{document}ΘL. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square $$\end{document}□

The Induced (Pre)symplectic Structure on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$B_i(S^1,\mathbb {R}^3)$$\end{document}Bi(S1,R3)

In the previous part, we have calculated a (pre)symplectic form on the space of parametrized curves \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{Imm}(S^1,\mathbb {R}^3)$$\end{document}Imm(S1,R3); we are, however, rather interested to construct symplectic structures on the shape space of geometric curves \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$B_i(S^1,\mathbb {R}^3)$$\end{document}Bi(S1,R3). The following result contains necessary and sufficient conditions for the forms \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Theta ^L$$\end{document}ΘL and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Omega ^L$$\end{document}ΩL to descend to this quotient space:

Theorem 2.6

(The (pre)symplectic structure on unparametrized curves) The form \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Omega ^L$$\end{document}ΩL factors to a (pre)symplectic form \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\bar{\Omega }^L$$\end{document}Ω¯L on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$B_i(S^1,\mathbb {R}^3)$$\end{document}Bi(S1,R3) if the inertia operator L is equivariant under the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\operatorname {Diff}^+(S^1)$$\end{document}Diff+(S1)-action and maps vertical tangent vectors to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\operatorname {span}\{c,c_\theta \}$$\end{document}span{c,cθ}, i.e., if for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c\in \textrm{Imm}(S^1,\mathbb {R}^3)$$\end{document}c∈Imm(S1,R3) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a\in C^{\infty }(S^1)$$\end{document}a∈C∞(S1) we have \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L_c(a.c_\theta )=a_1 c_\theta +a_2 c$$\end{document}Lc(a.cθ)=a1cθ+a2c for some functions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a_i\in C^{\infty }(S^1)$$\end{document}ai∈C∞(S1).

Proof

The Liouville form \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Theta ^L$$\end{document}ΘL on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{Imm}(S^1,\mathbb {R}^3)$$\end{document}Imm(S1,R3) factors to a smooth 1-form Since \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} In that case also its exterior derivative satisfies\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Omega ^L=- d \Theta ^L = - d \pi ^* \bar{\Theta }^L = - \pi ^* d \bar{\Theta }^L =: \pi ^* \bar{\Omega }^L \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ΩL=-dΘL=-dπ∗Θ¯L=-π∗dΘ¯L=:π∗Ω¯Lfor the presymplectic form \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\bar{\Omega }^L = - d\bar{\Theta }^L$$\end{document}Ω¯L=-dΘ¯L on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$B_i(S^1,\mathbb {R}^3)$$\end{document}Bi(S1,R3). \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square $$\end{document}□

Example 2.7

(Inertia operators with a prescribed horizontal bundle) There are several different examples of operators that satisfy these conditions, including in particular the class of almost local metrics:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} L_c(h)&= F(c).h \text { for }F\in C^\infty (\textrm{Imm}(S^1,\mathbb {R}^3),\mathbb {R}_{>0}), \text { for example} \\ L_c (h)&= \Phi ({\ell (c)})h, \quad L_c (h) = \Phi (\int _{S^1} \frac{\kappa ^2}{2} \textrm{d}s)h, \quad L_c(h) = (1+ A\kappa ^2)h, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Lc(h)=F(c).hforF∈C∞(Imm(S1,R3),R>0),for exampleLc(h)=Φ(ℓ(c))h,Lc(h)=Φ(∫S1κ22ds)h,Lc(h)=(1+Aκ2)h,where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\kappa _c=|D_s^2c|$$\end{document}κc=|Ds2c| denotes the curvature and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Phi :{\mathbb {R}}_{\ge 0}\rightarrow {\mathbb {R}}_{>0}$$\end{document}Φ:R≥0→R>0 is a suitable smooth function. Note, that the class of Sobolev metrics, as introduced in Remark 2.1 does not satisfy the conditions of the above theorem. Thus these metrics do not induce a (pre)symplectic form on the quotient space. By including a projection operator in their definition one can, however, modify these higher-order metrics to still respect the vertical bundle:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} L_c h&= \Big (\operatorname {pr}_c(1- (-1)^k D_s^{2k})\operatorname {pr}_c + (1-\operatorname {pr}_c)(1- (-1)^k D_s^{2k})(1-\operatorname {pr}_c)\Big ) h, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Lch=(prc(1-(-1)kDs2k)prc+(1-prc)(1-(-1)kDs2k)(1-prc))h,where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\operatorname {pr}_c h= \langle D_sc, h\rangle D_sc$$\end{document}prch=⟨Dsc,h⟩Dsc is the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^2$$\end{document}L2-orthogonal projection to the vertical bundle. For more details see Bauer and Harms (2015), where metrics of this form were studied in detail.

Remark 2.8

(Horizontal \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Omega ^L$$\end{document}ΩL-Hamiltonian vector fields and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\bar{\Omega }^{L}$$\end{document}Ω¯L-Hamiltonian vector fields) In the following we assume that the inertia operator \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L \in \Gamma (\operatorname {End}(T\textrm{Imm}(S^1,\mathbb {R}^3))$$\end{document}L∈Γ(End(TImm(S1,R3)) induces a (weak) symplectic structure on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$B_i(S^1,\mathbb {R}^3)$$\end{document}Bi(S1,R3), i.e., it satisfies the conditions of Theorem 2.6 and is moreover weakly non-degenerate in the sense that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\bar{\Omega }^L: {TB_i(S^1,{\mathbb {R}}^3) \rightarrow T^*B_i(S^1,{\mathbb {R}}^3) }$$\end{document}Ω¯L:TBi(S1,R3)→T∗Bi(S1,R3) is injective. Since \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T_c^*\pi \circ \bar{\Omega }^L_{\pi (c)}\circ T_c\pi = \Omega ^L_c$$\end{document}Tc∗π∘Ω¯π(c)L∘Tcπ=ΩcL, this is equivalent to the kernel of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Omega ^L_c:T_c\textrm{Imm}\rightarrow T_c^*\textrm{Imm}$$\end{document}ΩcL:TcImm→Tc∗Imm being equal to the tangent space to the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\operatorname {Diff}^+(S^1)$$\end{document}Diff+(S1)-orbit \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c\circ \operatorname {Diff}^+(S^1)$$\end{document}c∘Diff+(S1) for all c. Thus \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Omega ^L_c$$\end{document}ΩcL restricted to the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G^L$$\end{document}GL-orthogonal complement of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T_c(c\circ \operatorname {Diff}^+(S^1))$$\end{document}Tc(c∘Diff+(S1)) is injective. See (Kriegl and Michor 1997, Section 48) for more details.

Assume that H is a \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\operatorname {Diff}^+(S^1)$$\end{document}Diff+(S1)-invariant smooth function on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{Imm}(S^1,\mathbb {R}^3)$$\end{document}Imm(S1,R3). Since the 2-form \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Omega ^L$$\end{document}ΩL on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{Imm}(S^1,\mathbb {R}^3)$$\end{document}Imm(S1,R3) is only presymplectic it does not directly define a Hamiltonian vector field. However, if each \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$dH_c$$\end{document}dHc lies in the image of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Omega ^L:T\textrm{Imm}(S^1,\mathbb {R}^3) \rightarrow T^*\textrm{Imm}(S^1,\mathbb {R}^3)$$\end{document}ΩL:TImm(S1,R3)→T∗Imm(S1,R3), then a unique smooth horizontal Hamiltonian vector field \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X\in \mathfrak {X}(\textrm{Imm})$$\end{document}X∈X(Imm) is determined by\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} dH = i_X\Omega ^L = \Omega ^L(X,\;) \text { and } G^L_c(X_c,Tc.Y)=0,\quad \forall Y\in \mathfrak {X}(S^1) \end{aligned}$$\end{document}dH=iXΩL=ΩL(X,)andGcL(Xc,Tc.Y)=0,∀Y∈X(S1)which we will denote by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\operatorname {hgrad}^{\Omega ^L}(H)$$\end{document}hgradL(H). Obviously we then have\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \operatorname {grad}^{\bar{\Omega }^L}(\bar{H})\circ \pi = T\pi \circ \operatorname {hgrad}^{\Omega ^L}(H). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}gradL(H¯)∘π=Tπ∘hgradL(H).Here and in the rest of the article, we write \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\operatorname {grad}^A E$$\end{document}gradAE for the vector field satisfying \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A(\operatorname {grad}^A E,\cdot )=dE$$\end{document}A(gradAE,·)=dE for a given non-degenerate bilinear form A such as a Riemannian metric and a symplectic form, and a function E.

Sometimes the kernel of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Omega ^L$$\end{document}ΩL will be larger than the tangent spaces to the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\operatorname {Diff}(S^1)$$\end{document}Diff(S1)-orbits; then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\operatorname {hgrad}^{\Omega ^L}(H)$$\end{document}hgradL(H) will be chosen \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G^L$$\end{document}GL-perpendicular to the kernel of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Omega ^L$$\end{document}ΩL. This will happen in Theorem 3.2, for example, where L is a function of c such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Theta _c^L$$\end{document}ΘcL is also invariant under scaling. The Hamiltonian H factors to the corresponding space \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{Imm}(S^1,\mathbb {R}^3)/\ker \Omega ^L$$\end{document}Imm(S1,R3)/kerΩL (which denotes the quotient by the foliation generated by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\ker \Omega ^L$$\end{document}kerΩL) if H is additionally invariant under each vector in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\ker \Omega ^L$$\end{document}kerΩL.

Remark 2.9

For the Marsden–Weinstein structure \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Omega ^{\operatorname {MW}}=-d\Theta ^{\frac{1}{3}\textrm{id}}$$\end{document}ΩMW=-dΘ13id, we have\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \operatorname {hgrad}^{\Omega ^{\operatorname {MW}}} H = - D_s c \times \operatorname {grad}^{G^\textrm{id}} H \end{aligned}$$\end{document}hgradMWH=-Dsc×gradidHsince\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} G^\textrm{id}(D_s c \times \cdot , \cdot )=\Omega ^{\operatorname {MW}}(\cdot ,\cdot ). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Gid(Dsc×·,·)=ΩMW(·,·).

Remark 2.10

(Momentum mappings) If a Lie group \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {G}$$\end{document}G acts on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{Imm}(S^1,{\mathbb {R}}^3)$$\end{document}Imm(S1,R3) and preserves \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Theta ^L$$\end{document}ΘL, the corresponding momentum mapping J can be expressed in terms of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Theta ^L$$\end{document}ΘL and the fundamental vector field mapping \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\zeta :\mathfrak g \rightarrow \mathfrak {X}(\textrm{Imm}(S^1,\mathbb {R}^3))$$\end{document}ζ:g→X(Imm(S1,R3)). For \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Y\in \mathfrak g$$\end{document}Y∈g, we have\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \langle J(c),Y\rangle = \Theta ^L(\zeta _Y)_c = \int \langle c\times D_sc, L_c\zeta _Y\rangle \textrm{d}s, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}⟨J(c),Y⟩=ΘL(ζY)c=∫⟨c×Dsc,LcζY⟩ds,where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\langle \cdot ,\cdot \rangle : \mathfrak g^* \times \mathfrak g\rightarrow \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}⟨·,·⟩:g∗×g→R is the duality product and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\zeta _Y$$\end{document}ζY is the fundamental vector field generated by Y. Namely,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} d \Theta ^L(\zeta _Y)&= d i_{\zeta _Y} \Theta ^L = \mathcal {L}_{\zeta _Y} \Theta ^L - i_{\zeta _Y} d\Theta ^L = 0 - i_{\zeta _Y} \Omega ^L. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}dΘL(ζY)=diζYΘL=LζYΘL-iζYdΘL=0-iζYΩL.Lemma 2.2 asserts that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Theta ^L$$\end{document}ΘL is invariant under the right action of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\operatorname {Diff}^+(S^1)$$\end{document}Diff+(S1) and the left action of SO(3).

Thus for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X=a.\partial _\theta \in \mathfrak {X}(S^1)= C^\infty (S^1)\partial _\theta $$\end{document}X=a.∂θ∈X(S1)=C∞(S1)∂θ the reparameterization momentum is given as follows:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \zeta _{a.\partial _\theta }(c)&= D_{c,a.c_\theta } \qquad \text {as derivation at }c\text { on } C^\infty (\textrm{Imm}, \mathbb {R}) \\ &= a. c_\theta = a.|c_\theta | D_s c \in T_c\textrm{Imm}= C^\infty (S^1,\mathbb {R}^3) \\ L_{c\circ \varphi }(h\circ \varphi )&= (L_ch)\circ \varphi \implies (D_{c,a.c_\theta } L_c)(h) + L_c(a.h_\theta ) = a.(L_ch)_\theta \\ \langle J^{\operatorname {Diff}^+(S^1)}(c), a.\partial _\theta \rangle&= \Theta ^L_c(\zeta _{a.\partial _\theta }(c)) = \Theta ^L_c (a.c_\theta ) = \int \langle c\times D_sc, L_c(a.c_\theta )\rangle \textrm{d}s \\ &= \int \langle c\times D_sc, a.(L_cc)_\theta - If L is also invariant under translations, then the linear momentum, for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$y\in \mathbb {R}^3$$\end{document}y∈R3, is\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \langle J^{\mathbb {R}^3}(c), y\rangle&= \Theta ^L_c(y) = \int \langle c\times D_sc, L_c(y)\rangle \textrm{d}s\,. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}⟨J3(c),y⟩=ΘcL(y)=∫⟨c×Dsc,Lc(y)⟩ds.Note that the above also furnishes conserved quantities on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$B_i$$\end{document}Bi, if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\bar{\Omega }^L$$\end{document}Ω¯L is non-degenerate.

Symplectic Structures Induced by Conformal Factors

In this section we consider symplectic structures induced by Riemannian metrics, that are conformally equivalent to the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^2$$\end{document}L2-metric, i.e., we consider the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G^L$$\end{document}GL metric for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L_c=\lambda (c)$$\end{document}Lc=λ(c) where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda :\textrm{Imm}(S^1,{\mathbb {R}}^3)\rightarrow {\mathbb {R}}_{>0}$$\end{document}λ:Imm(S1,R3)→R>0 is invariant under reparametrization. Thus \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda $$\end{document}λ factors to a function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\bar{\lambda }:B_i(S^1,{\mathbb {R}}^3)\rightarrow {\mathbb {R}}_{>0}$$\end{document}λ¯:Bi(S1,R3)→R>0 by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\pi ^* \bar{\lambda }= {\bar{\lambda }\circ \pi =}\lambda $$\end{document}π∗λ¯=λ¯∘π=λ. Moreover, if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\operatorname {grad}^{G^\textrm{id}}_c\lambda $$\end{document}gradcidλ exists (which we assume) it is pointwise perpendicular to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$D_s c$$\end{document}Dsc.

We first study the scale invariance of the corresponding Liouville 1-form, which will be of importance for the calculation of the induced (pre)symplectic structure. We say \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Theta ^L$$\end{document}ΘL is scale-invariant at \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c\in \textrm{Imm}(S^1,{\mathbb {R}}^3)$$\end{document}c∈Imm(S1,R3) if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {L}_I \Theta ^L_c=0$$\end{document}LIΘcL=0 where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$I\in \Gamma (T\textrm{Imm}(S^1,{\mathbb {R}}^3))$$\end{document}I∈Γ(TImm(S1,R3)) is the scaling vector field \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$I_c :=c$$\end{document}Ic:=c with flow \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\operatorname {Fl}^I_t(c)= e^t.c$$\end{document}FltI(c)=et.c. Depending on the context, we use both I and c for scaling as a tangent vector in this article.

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda (c) = \Lambda (c/\ell (c)).\ell (c)^{-3}$$\end{document}λ(c)=Λ(c/ℓ(c)).ℓ(c)-3 for a smooth function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Lambda : \{c\in \textrm{Imm}:\ell (c)=1\}\rightarrow \mathbb {R}_{>0}$$\end{document}Λ:{c∈Imm:ℓ(c)=1}→R>0,

Proof

If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$3{\lambda (c)} + (\mathcal {L}_I\lambda ){(c)}=3\lambda (c)+D_{c,c}\lambda (c)\ne 0$$\end{document}3λ(c)+(LIλ)(c)=3λ(c)+Dc,cλ(c)≠0 on any open subset of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{Imm}(S^1,{\mathbb {R}}^3)$$\end{document}Imm(S1,R3), then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Omega ^\lambda $$\end{document}Ωλ induces a non-degenerate 2-form on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$B_i(S^1,{\mathbb {R}}^3)$$\end{document}Bi(S1,R3), which is thus symplectic.

If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$3{\lambda (c)} + (\mathcal {L}_I\lambda ){(c)}=3\lambda (c)+D_{c,c}\lambda (c)\ne 0$$\end{document}3λ(c)+(LIλ)(c)=3λ(c)+Dc,cλ(c)≠0 on any open subset of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{Imm}(S^1,{\mathbb {R}}^3)$$\end{document}Imm(S1,R3), then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Omega ^\lambda $$\end{document}Ωλ induces a non-degenerate 2-form on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$B_i(S^1,{\mathbb {R}}^3)$$\end{document}Bi(S1,R3), which is thus symplectic.

Assume in addition, that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X:=\operatorname {hgrad}^{\Omega ^\textrm{id}}\lambda $$\end{document}X:=hgradidλ exists, is smooth, admits a flow, and that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$3\lambda {(c)} + (\mathcal {L}_I\lambda ){(c)} =0$$\end{document}3λ(c)+(LIλ)(c)=0 for all c. If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {L}_X\ell = 0$$\end{document}LXℓ=0, it is also non-degenerate on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{\bar{c}\in B_i(S^1,{\mathbb {R}}^3): \bar{\ell }_{\bar{c}}=1\}/\operatorname {span}(\operatorname {grad}^{\bar{\Omega }^\textrm{id}}\bar{\lambda })$$\end{document}{c¯∈Bi(S1,R3):ℓ¯c¯=1}/span(gradidλ¯) where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\ell =\bar{\ell }\circ \pi $$\end{document}ℓ=ℓ¯∘π denotes the length function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\bar{\ell }$$\end{document}ℓ¯ on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$B_i(S^1,{\mathbb {R}}^3)$$\end{document}Bi(S1,R3).

Assume in addition, that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X:=\operatorname {hgrad}^{\Omega ^\textrm{id}}\lambda $$\end{document}X:=hgradidλ exists, is smooth, admits a flow, and that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$3\lambda {(c)} + (\mathcal {L}_I\lambda ){(c)} =0$$\end{document}3λ(c)+(LIλ)(c)=0 for all c.
