Remark 3.3

(Smooth structure of the orbit space) In case (b), the vector field \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X:=\operatorname {hgrad}^{\Omega ^\textrm{id}}\lambda $$\end{document}X:=hgradidλ exists in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {X}(\textrm{Imm}(S^1,{\mathbb {R}}^3))$$\end{document}X(Imm(S1,R3)) if and only if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\operatorname {grad}^{G^{\operatorname {id}}}\lambda $$\end{document}gradidλ exists and is smooth as we have \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\operatorname {hgrad}^{\Omega ^{\operatorname {id}}}\lambda = \operatorname {hgrad}^{3\Omega ^{\operatorname {MW}}}\lambda = -\frac{1}{3}D_s c \times \operatorname {grad}^{G^\textrm{id}}\lambda $$\end{document}hgradidλ=hgrad3ΩMWλ=-13Dsc×gradidλ. This is equivalent to the fact that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\bar{\lambda }\in C^\infty (B_i(S^1,\mathbb {R}^3),\mathbb {R})$$\end{document}λ¯∈C∞(Bi(S1,R3),R) by B.4. Moreover, the vector fields I and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\operatorname {hgrad}^{\Omega ^\textrm{id}}\lambda (=\frac{1}{3}\operatorname {hgrad}^{\Omega ^{\operatorname {MW}}}\lambda )$$\end{document}hgradidλ(=13hgradMWλ) are linearly independent at any c because \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Omega ^{\textrm{id}}_c(\operatorname {hgrad}^{\Omega ^\textrm{id}}_c\lambda , I_c) = i_I d\lambda (c) = -3\lambda (c) \ne 0$$\end{document}Ωcid(hgradcidλ,Ic)=iIdλ(c)=-3λ(c)≠0 by assumption. So the dimension of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {F}$$\end{document}F is always 2. We project to the leaf space of the two-dimensional distribution if it is integrable. This is the case, if the flow of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X=\operatorname {hgrad}^{\Omega ^\textrm{id}}_c\lambda $$\end{document}X=hgradcidλ and thus also of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\operatorname {grad}^{\bar{\Omega }^\textrm{id}}\bar{\lambda }$$\end{document}gradidλ¯ exists; then the flows of I and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\operatorname {grad}^{\bar{\Omega }^\textrm{id}}\bar{\lambda }$$\end{document}gradidλ¯ combine to a two-dimensional \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(ax+b)$$\end{document}(ax+b)-group acting on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{Imm}(S^1,{\mathbb {R}}^3)$$\end{document}Imm(S1,R3). We assume that this is the case; to prove existence of the flow one has first to specify \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda $$\end{document}λ and then solve a non-linear PDE.

Furthermore, we note that the smooth structure of the corresponding quotient space \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{Imm}(S^1,\mathbb {R}^3)/(\operatorname {Diff}^+(S^1)\times \mathcal {F}$$\end{document}Imm(S1,R3)/(Diff+(S1)×F is slightly subtle: It is always a Frölicher space with tangent bundle; see (Kriegl and Michor 1997, Section 23). If local smooth sections of the projection to the leaf space exist and if the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(ax+b)$$\end{document}(ax+b)-orbits admit slices, then we get a principal bundle with structure group the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(ax+b)$$\end{document}(ax+b)-group in the category of orbifolds, so the leaf space is also an orbifold.

Proof

The formula directly follows from the product rule applied to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$d(\Theta ^\lambda )=d(\lambda \Theta ^\textrm{id})$$\end{document}d(Θλ)=d(λΘid).

Case (a): We now show the non-degeneracy; if a tangent vector h satisfies \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h\perp D_s c$$\end{document}h⊥Dsc pointwise and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Omega ^\lambda _c(h,k)= 0$$\end{document}Ωcλ(h,k)=0 for any k, then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h=0$$\end{document}h=0. First, choosing \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k=a.c$$\end{document}k=a.c with some non-zero constant \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a\in {\mathbb {R}}^\times $$\end{document}a∈R× we get from \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a.c\in \ker \Theta ^\textrm{id}$$\end{document}a.c∈kerΘid that,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} 0=\Omega ^\lambda _c(h,ac)=\lambda \Omega ^\textrm{id}(h,ac)+\Theta ^\textrm{id}(h)i_{a.c}d\lambda -0=a [3\lambda + D_{c,c}\lambda ]\Theta ^\textrm{id}(h). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}0=Ωcλ(h,ac)=λΩid(h,ac)+Θid(h)ia.cdλ-0=a[3λ+Dc,cλ]Θid(h).With our assumption \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$3\lambda + D_{c,c}\lambda \ne 0$$\end{document}3λ+Dc,cλ≠0 we see \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h\in \ker \Theta ^\textrm{id}$$\end{document}h∈kerΘid.

Next, we test for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h\in \ker \Theta ^\textrm{id}$$\end{document}h∈kerΘid and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k=a.c$$\end{document}k=a.c with some function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a\in C^\infty (S^1)$$\end{document}a∈C∞(S1) to see\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Omega ^\lambda _c(h,a.c) =\lambda \Omega ^\textrm{id}(h,a.c) =3\lambda \int a\langle c \times D_s c, h\rangle \textrm{d}s. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Ωcλ(h,a.c)=λΩid(h,a.c)=3λ∫a⟨c×Dsc,h⟩ds.If this vanishes for any function a, we have \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\langle c \times D_s c, h\rangle =0$$\end{document}⟨c×Dsc,h⟩=0 everywhere. Then we have\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Omega ^\lambda _c(h,k)&=\lambda \Omega ^\textrm{id}(h,k)+\Theta ^\textrm{id}(h)i_k d\lambda -\Theta ^\textrm{id}(k)i_h d\lambda \\&=\lambda \Omega ^\textrm{id}(b.c,k)+0-\Theta ^\textrm{id}(k)i_{b.c}d\lambda \\&\quad + \lambda \Omega ^\textrm{id}(v,k)+0-\Theta ^\textrm{id}(k)i_v d\lambda \\&=\int _{S^1} \langle (3\lambda .b + D_{c,b.c}\lambda + D_{c,v}\lambda ) D_s c \times c+ 3 \lambda . Hence, we have \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v\equiv 0$$\end{document}v≡0. With this, we have\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Omega ^\lambda _c(h,k)=\int _{U} (3\lambda .b + D_{c,b.c}\lambda ) \langle D_s c \times c, k\rangle \textrm{d}s. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Ωcλ(h,k)=∫U(3λ.b+Dc,b.cλ)⟨Dsc×c,k⟩ds.In order that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \Omega ^\lambda _c(h,k)=0$$\end{document}Ωcλ(h,k)=0 for any k, we must have \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$3\lambda .b + D_{c,b.c}\lambda \equiv 0$$\end{document}3λ.b+Dc,b.cλ≡0 on U. (i)The open subset \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$U = \{\theta \in S^1: c(\theta )\times D_sc(\theta )\ne 0 \}$$\end{document}U={θ∈S1:c(θ)×Dsc(θ)≠0},

Remark 3.4

(Symplectic reduction) Our reduction of the space \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$B_i(S^1,{\mathbb {R}}^3)$$\end{document}Bi(S1,R3) in the second case of Theorem 3.2 can be seen as an infinite-dimensional instance of the Marsden–Weinstein-Meyer symplectic reduction. To see this, let us set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\bar{X}:=\operatorname {grad}_{\bar{c}}^{\bar{\Omega }^{\operatorname {id}}} \bar{\lambda }$$\end{document}X¯:=gradc¯idλ¯ and take the momentum map \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\bar{J}:B_i(S^1,{\mathbb {R}}^3)\rightarrow {\mathbb {R}}$$\end{document}J¯:Bi(S1,R3)→R by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\bar{J}(\bar{c}):=\bar{\lambda }(\bar{c})$$\end{document}J¯(c¯):=λ¯(c¯), with the corresponding group action being the time-t flow of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\bar{X}$$\end{document}X¯ with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\bar{c}$$\end{document}c¯ as initial data. We have shown that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\bar{\Omega }^\lambda $$\end{document}Ω¯λ is degenerate on the codimension-1 suborbifold \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\bar{J}^{-1}(1)=\{\bar{c} \in B_i(S^1,{\mathbb {R}}^3) \mid \bar{\lambda }(\bar{c})=1\}$$\end{document}J¯-1(1)={c¯∈Bi(S1,R3)∣λ¯(c¯)=1}, and that it becomes symplectic when factored onto the codimension-2 suborbifold \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\bar{J}^{-1}(1)/ \operatorname {grad}^{\bar{\Omega }^{\operatorname {id}}} \bar{\lambda }$$\end{document}J¯-1(1)/gradidλ¯.

We also remark that the dual product for the momentum map \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\bar{J}$$\end{document}J¯ is just the multiplication of scalar values as we have\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \langle \bar{J}(\bar{c}), t\rangle = -\bar{\Theta }_{\bar{c}}^\textrm{id}(t.\bar{X})=t. \bar{\lambda }(\bar{c}) \end{aligned}$$\end{document}⟨J¯(c¯),t⟩=-Θ¯c¯id(t.X¯)=t.λ¯(c¯)for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t\in {\mathbb {R}}$$\end{document}t∈R such that the time-t flow map of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\bar{X}$$\end{document}X¯ exists. Here, we used the invariance of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\bar{\Theta }_{\bar{c}}^\textrm{id}$$\end{document}Θ¯c¯id under the flow of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\bar{X}$$\end{document}X¯, which is shown by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {L}_{\bar{X}} \bar{\Theta }_{\bar{c}}^\textrm{id}=d i_{\bar{X}} \bar{\Theta }_{\bar{c}}^\textrm{id}+ i_{\bar{X}} \bar{\Omega }_{\bar{c}}^\textrm{id}= -d\bar{\lambda } +d\bar{\lambda }=0$$\end{document}LX¯Θ¯c¯id=diX¯Θ¯c¯id+iX¯Ω¯c¯id=-dλ¯+dλ¯=0 mimicking computations in the proof of Theorem 3.2. We may get the same result also using \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\bar{\Theta }^\lambda $$\end{document}Θ¯λ and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \operatorname {grad}_{\bar{c}}^{\bar{\Omega }^\lambda } \bar{\lambda } = T_c \pi (\operatorname {hgrad}_c^{\Omega ^\lambda } \lambda )$$\end{document}gradc¯λλ¯=Tcπ(hgradcλλ) (cf. Proposition 3.6) instead of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\bar{\Theta }^{\textrm{id}}$$\end{document}Θ¯id and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\bar{X}= \operatorname {grad}_{\bar{c}}^{\bar{\Omega }^{\operatorname {id}}} \bar{\lambda }$$\end{document}X¯=gradc¯idλ¯.

Remark 3.5

(A pseudo-Riemannian metric via \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Omega ^L$$\end{document}ΩL and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} Note that such \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\bar{R}$$\end{document}R¯ is different from the Riemannian metric \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G^L$$\end{document}GL factored onto \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$B_i(S^1,{\mathbb {R}}^3)$$\end{document}Bi(S1,R3) we used to define the Liouville form \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Theta ^L$$\end{document}ΘL.

We here compute \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\bar{R}$$\end{document}R¯ for the conformal factor \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L_c=\lambda (c)$$\end{document}Lc=λ(c). In the computation, we identify the tangent space at \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\bar{c}}$$\end{document}c¯ of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$B_i(S^1,\mathbb {R}^3)$$\end{document}Bi(S1,R3) with the space of tangent vectors h in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T_c\textrm{Imm}(S^1,\mathbb {R}^3)$$\end{document}TcImm(S1,R3), such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\langle D_sc,h\rangle =0$$\end{document}⟨Dsc,h⟩=0, and denote \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J=D_s c\times \cdot $$\end{document}J=Dsc×·.

We then have\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {\bar{R}_{\bar{c}}}({\bar{h},\bar{k}})&:=\Omega ^{\lambda }_c(h,{J} k) =\lambda (c)\Omega _c^{\operatorname {id}}(h,{J} k) +\Theta _c(h) \mathcal {L}_{{J} k} \lambda (c)-\Theta _c({J} k) \mathcal {L}_h \lambda (c). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}R¯c¯(h¯,k¯):=Ωcλ(h,Jk)=λ(c)Ωcid(h,Jk)+Θc(h)LJkλ(c)-Θc(Jk)Lhλ(c).By design \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\bar{R}}$$\end{document}R¯ is non-degenerate. The symmetry follows from \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Omega _c^\lambda ({J} h,k)=-\Omega _c^\lambda (h,{J} k)$$\end{document}Ωcλ(Jh,k)=-Ωcλ(h,Jk). It is, however, not clear if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\bar{R}$$\end{document}R¯ is positive-definite, i.e., if it is a Riemannian metric. We leave this question open for future research.

Hamiltonian Vector Fields

Now we compute the horizontal Hamiltonian vector field \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\operatorname {hgrad}^{\Omega ^\lambda }H$$\end{document}hgradλH for a given reparametrization-invariant Hamiltonian H. We express \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\operatorname {hgrad}^{\Omega ^\lambda }H$$\end{document}hgradλH in terms of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\operatorname {grad}^{G^\textrm{id}}H$$\end{document}gradidH since the latter is in general relatively easy to obtain.

Proof

Let us denote for simplicity \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A:=\operatorname {grad}^{G^\textrm{id}}\lambda $$\end{document}A:=gradidλ and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X_H:=\operatorname {hgrad}^{\Omega ^\lambda }H$$\end{document}XH:=hgradλH. We can isolate out k from \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Omega ^\lambda _c (X_H,k)$$\end{document}Ωcλ(XH,k) by\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Omega ^\lambda (X_H,k)&=\lambda \Omega ^\textrm{id}(X_H,k)+\Theta ^\textrm{id}(X_H)D_{c,k}\lambda - \Theta ^\textrm{id}(k)D_{c,X_H}\lambda \\&=\int _{S^1} \langle 3\lambda . D_s c \times X_H - D_{c,X_H}\lambda . c \times D_s c +\Theta ^\textrm{id}(X_H) A, k\rangle \textrm{d}s. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Ωλ(XH,k)=λΩid(XH,k)+Θid(XH)Dc,kλ-Θid(k)Dc,XHλ=∫S1⟨3λ.Dsc×XH-Dc,XHλ.c×Dsc+Θid(XH)A,k⟩ds.Using \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Omega ^\lambda _c (X_H,k)=dH(k)=G^\textrm{id}(\operatorname {grad}^{G^\textrm{id}} H,k)$$\end{document}Ωcλ(XH,k)=dH(k)=Gid(gradidH,k), we get\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} 0&= \Omega ^\lambda (X_H,k) - dH(k)\\&=\int _{S^1} \langle 3\lambda . D_s c \times X_H - D_{c,X_H}\lambda . c \times D_s c +\Theta ^\textrm{id}(X_H) A - \operatorname {grad}^{G^\textrm{id}} H, k\rangle \textrm{d}s. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}0=Ωλ(XH,k)-dH(k)=∫S1⟨3λ.Dsc×XH-Dc,XHλ.c×Dsc+Θid(XH)A-gradidH,k⟩ds.This must be satisfied for any k, namely we have3\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} 3\lambda .D_s c \times X_H - D_{c,X_H}\lambda . c \times D_s c +\Theta ^\textrm{id}(X_H) A - \operatorname {grad}^{G^\textrm{id}} H=0. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}3λ.Dsc×XH-Dc,XHλ.c×Dsc+Θid(XH)A-gradidH=0.Our goal is to solve this for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X_H$$\end{document}XH. Applying \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$- D_s c \times $$\end{document}-Dsc× reads\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} 3\lambda . X_H - D_{c,X_H}\lambda . D_s c \times ( D_s c \times c) -\Theta ^\textrm{id}(X_H) D_s c\times A + D_s c \times \operatorname {grad}^{G^\textrm{id}} H=0. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}3λ.XH-Dc,XHλ.Dsc×(Dsc×c)-Θid(XH)Dsc×A+Dsc×gradidH=0.Let us set4\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} X_H=\frac{-1}{3\lambda } D_s c \times \operatorname {grad}^{G^\textrm{id}} H + K_1 D_s c \times ( D_s c \times c) +K_2 D_s c \times A_c \end{aligned}$$\end{document}XH=-13λDsc×gradidH+K1Dsc×(Dsc×c)+K2Dsc×Acwith some coefficients \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$K_1, K_2$$\end{document}K1,K2 to be determined.

From\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} D_{c,X_H}\lambda =\int \langle A_c,X_H\rangle \textrm{d}s, \quad \Theta ^\textrm{id}(X_H)=\int \langle c\times D_s c, X_H \rangle \textrm{d}s, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Dc,XHλ=∫⟨Ac,XH⟩ds,Θid(XH)=∫⟨c×Dsc,XH⟩ds,we get5\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} 0&=3 \lambda K_1. D_s c \times (D_s c \times c) + 3\lambda K_2. D_s c \times A_c \nonumber \\&\qquad - \int \langle A_c, \frac{-1}{3\lambda } D_s c \times \operatorname {grad}^{G^\textrm{id}} H + K_1 D_s c \times ( D_s c \times c) \rangle \textrm{d}s. D_s c \times (D_s c \times c)\nonumber \\&\qquad - \int \langle A_c, \frac{-1}{3\lambda } D_s c \times \operatorname {grad}^{G^\textrm{id}} H + K_2 D_s c \times A_c\rangle \textrm{d}s. D_s c \times A_c\nonumber \\&= \Big [ K_1 \left( 3\lambda -\int \langle A_c, D_sc \times (D_s c \times c)\rangle \textrm{d}s \right) \nonumber \\ &\qquad \qquad \qquad +\frac{1}{3\lambda }\int \langle A_c,D_s c \times \operatorname {grad}^{G^\textrm{id}} H\rangle \textrm{d}s \Big ] D_s c \times (D_s c \times c) \nonumber \\ &\qquad + \Big [ K_2 \left( 3\lambda +\int \langle D_ s c \times c, D_sc \times A_c \rangle \textrm{d}s \right) \nonumber \\ &\qquad -\frac{1}{3\lambda }\int \langle D_s c \times c, D_s c \times \operatorname {grad}^{G^\textrm{id}} H\rangle \textrm{d}s \Big ] D_s c \times A_c \nonumber \\ &=\left[ K_1 \left( 3\lambda +D_{c,c}\lambda \right) +\frac{1}{3\lambda }\int \langle A_c,D_s c \times \operatorname {grad}^{G^\textrm{id}} H\rangle \textrm{d}s \right] D_s c \times (D_s c \times c) \nonumber \\ &\qquad + \left[ K_2 \left( 3\lambda +D_{c,c}\lambda \right) -\frac{1}{3\lambda }\int \langle D_s c \times c, D_s c \times \operatorname {grad}^{G^\textrm{id}} H\rangle Symplectic Structures Induced by Length Weighted Metrics

Next we study a special class of symplectic structures induced by conformal factors introduced in the previous section; namely we consider length-weighted metrics as studied in Yezzi and Mennucci (2005); Michor and Mumford (2006); Shah (2008). More precisely, we consider operators of the form \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L_c=\Phi ({\ell (c)})$$\end{document}Lc=Φ(ℓ(c)) where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\ell (c)}=\int _{S^1}|c_\theta |\textrm{d}\theta $$\end{document}ℓ(c)=∫S1|cθ|dθ denotes the length of the curve c and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Phi : \mathbb {R}_{> 0}\rightarrow \mathbb {R}_{> 0}$$\end{document}Φ:R>0→R>0 is a suitable function.

Using Theorem 3.2 we obtain the following result concerning the induced symplectic structure \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Omega ^{\Phi (\ell )}$$\end{document}ΩΦ(ℓ):

Proof

To calculate the formula for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Omega ^{\Phi (\ell )}$$\end{document}ΩΦ(ℓ) we first need to calculate the variation of the length \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\ell (c)}$$\end{document}ℓ(c). Now we will compute Hamiltonian vector fields. Therefore, we note that the conditions of Remark 2.8 are satisfied, which allows us to obtain the following result:

Proof

The stated formula follows from Proposition 3.6 with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\operatorname {grad}_c^{G^\textrm{id}}\Phi ({\ell (c)})=-\Phi '({\ell (c)})D_s^2 c$$\end{document}gradcidΦ(ℓ(c))=-Φ′(ℓ(c))Ds2c and that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\operatorname {hgrad}^{\Omega ^{\operatorname {MW}}}C\ell ^{p}$$\end{document}hgradMWCℓp is a constant multiple of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\operatorname {hgrad}^{\Omega ^{\operatorname {MW}}}\ell $$\end{document}hgradMWℓ. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square $$\end{document}□

Remark 4.3

From the above Proposition it follows \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\operatorname {hgrad}^{\Omega ^{\Phi (\ell )}}H$$\end{document}hgradΦ(ℓ)H agrees with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\operatorname {hgrad}^{\Omega ^{\operatorname {id}}}H$$\end{document}hgradidH up to a constant scaling if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Phi '({\ell (c)})=0$$\end{document}Φ′(ℓ(c))=0. If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Phi '({\ell (c)})\ne 0$$\end{document}Φ′(ℓ(c))≠0 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\langle D_s^2 c, \operatorname {grad}^{\Omega ^{\operatorname {id}}}H\rangle _{L^2_{\textrm{d}s}(S^1)}\ne 0$$\end{document}⟨Ds2c,gradidH⟩Lds2(S1)≠0 then it is, however, genuinely different, i.e., it does not seem realizable as a Hamiltonian vector field for the Marsden–Weinstein form \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Omega ^{\operatorname {MW}}$$\end{document}ΩMW. To formally prove that a given vector field \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X_H$$\end{document}XH is never attained by the Marsden–Weinstein structure one needs to show that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {L}_{X_H}\Omega ^{\operatorname {MW}}\ne 0$$\end{document}LXHΩMW≠0. Using the closeness of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Omega ^{\operatorname {MW}}$$\end{document}ΩMW and Cartan’s formula, this can be reduced to show that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$di_{X_H}\Omega ^{\operatorname {MW}}\ne 0$$\end{document}diXHΩMW≠0. However the necessary computations for this turn out to become extremely cumbersome and not very insightful. We refrain from providing them here.

Next we will consider several explicit examples, that will further highlight the statement of the above remark. We acknowledge that many of the Hamiltonian functions we consider were studied for the Marsden–Weinstein structure in Chern et al. (2020).

Example 4.4

(Length function) We start with the arguably simplest Hamiltonian, namely we assume that H is a function of the total length \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\ell $$\end{document}ℓ, i.e., \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H(c)=f\circ \ell (c)$$\end{document}H(c)=f∘ℓ(c) for some function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f$$\end{document}f. In this case we calculate:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} dH_c (k)= d [f\circ \ell ] _c(k) = D_{c,k}f({\ell (c)}) = f'({\ell (c)})\int \langle D_sk, D_sc\rangle If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f'({\ell (c)})\ne 0$$\end{document}f′(ℓ(c))≠0, then the length \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\ell (c)}$$\end{document}ℓ(c) is conserved along the flow as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H=f\circ \ell $$\end{document}H=f∘ℓ is conserved. Thus we have seen that the Hamiltonian vector field of the symplectic structure \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Omega ^{\Phi (\ell )}$$\end{document}ΩΦ(ℓ) is a constant multiple of the Hamiltonian vector field of the Marsden–Weinstein symplectic structure. Note, that this constant factor, i.e., the relative speed with respect to the standard binormal equation, depends on the initial length \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\ell (c)}$$\end{document}ℓ(c).

Example 4.5

(Flux of a divergence-free vector field on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathbb {R}}^3$$\end{document}R3 though a Seifert surface) Our next examples of Hamiltonians are the fluxes of vector fields through Seifert surfaces. \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} H\rangle _{L^2_{\textrm{d}s}(S^1)}&= \langle D_s c, D_s c \times D_s^2 c\rangle _{L^2_{\textrm{d}s}(S^1)}+\langle c, D_s^2 c \times D_s^2 c\rangle _{L^2_{\textrm{d}s}(S^1)}= 0+0. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}⟨Ds2c,Dsc×gradidH⟩Lds2(S1)=-12∫Dsκ2ds=0⟨c,gradidH⟩Lds2(S1)=⟨Dsc,Dsc×Ds2c⟩Lds2(S1)+⟨c,Ds2c×Ds2c⟩Lds2(S1)=0+0.Then we get\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \operatorname {hgrad}^{\Omega ^{\Phi (\ell )}} H&=\frac{1}{3\Phi ({\ell (c)})} D_s c\times (D_s c\times D_s^3 c) =\frac{1}{3\Phi ({\ell (c)})}\operatorname {hgrad}^{\Omega ^{\operatorname {MW}}}H , \end{aligned}$$\end{document}hgradΦ(ℓ)H=13Φ(ℓ(c))Dsc×(Dsc×Ds3c)=13Φ(ℓ(c))hgradMWH,which is a scaled version of the Marsden–Weinstein gradient flow.

Example 4.8

(Squared scale) Next we consider the squared scale\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} E(c):=\frac{1}{2} \int |c|^2 \textrm{d}s, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}E(c):=12∫|c|2ds,as a Hamiltonian function. We first get by a direct computation that,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \operatorname {grad}^{G^\textrm{id}}E&=c - Example 4.9

(Product of length and total squared curvature) Our last example is the Hamiltonian given by\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} H(c)={\ell (c)} K(c) \end{aligned}$$\end{document}H(c)=ℓ(c)K(c)where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$K(c)=\int _{S^1} \kappa ^2 \textrm{d}s$$\end{document}K(c)=∫S1κ2ds is the total squared curvature. This somewhat unusual Hamiltonian is the only one among our examples that satisfies the condition required in Corollary 4.2 (b) the scale-invariant case. That is, H is invariant under the both flows of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$I=c$$\end{document}I=c and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Y:=\operatorname {hgrad}^{\Omega ^{\operatorname {MW}}}\ell =D_s c\times D_s^2 c$$\end{document}Y:=hgradMWℓ=Dsc×Ds2c. To see this, let us compute\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {L}_{Y}H =K\mathcal {L}_Y \ell +\ell \mathcal {L}_Y K =K\cdot 0+\ell \cdot 0 =0 \end{aligned}$$\end{document}LYH=KLYℓ+ℓLYK=K·0+ℓ·0=0as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\ell $$\end{document}ℓ is the Hamiltonian of Y and the last equality follows from a direct computation using (10). This shows the existence of a Hamiltonian vector field horizontal in the sense of Corollary 4.2 (b).

OpenProblem 4.10

We know from the above examples that some vector fields are realized as Hamiltonian vector fields of both \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\bar{\Omega }^{\operatorname {MW}}$$\end{document}Ω¯MW and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\bar{\Omega }^{\Phi (\ell )}$$\end{document}Ω¯Φ(ℓ). We still do not know whether the spaces of all Hamiltonian vector fields generated by these two symplectic structures coincide, or if one is contained in the other. More generally, the coverage of Hamiltonian vector fields of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\bar{\Omega }^{L}$$\end{document}Ω¯L for a given operator L is an independent question, which we have not investigated in this article.

Presymplectic Structures Induced by Curvature Weighted Riemannian Metrics

In this section we will consider the special case of symplectic structures, that are induced by curvature-weighted metrics, i.e., we consider the Riemannian metric\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} G^{1+\kappa ^2}_c(h,k)=\int _{S^1}(1+\kappa ^2)\langle h,k\rangle \textrm{d}s, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Gc1+κ2(h,k)=∫S1(1+κ2)⟨h,k⟩ds,where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\kappa =\kappa _c$$\end{document}κ=κc denotes the curvature of the curve c. Note, that in the notation of the previous sections, this metric corresponds to the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G^L$$\end{document}GL metric with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L=1+\kappa ^2$$\end{document}L=1+κ2. This metric, which is sometimes also called the Michor-Mumford metric, has been originally introduced in Michor and Mumford (2006) to overcome the vanishing distance phenomenon of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^2$$\end{document}L2-metric (see also Michor and Mumford (2005)).

Remark 5.1

(Relations to the Frenet-Serret formulas) Given \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c\in \textrm{Imm}(S^1,\mathbb {R}^3)$$\end{document}c∈Imm(S1,R3) we consider the open subset \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$U =\{ \kappa >0\} = \{D_s^2c\ne 0\}\subset S^1$$\end{document}U={κ>0}={Ds2c≠0}⊂S1. Remark 5.2

Similarly to Remark 2.10 we obtain again conserved quantities and corresponding momentum mappings. Here we want to specifically highlight the momentum map \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J^{SO(3)}$$\end{document}JSO(3): as an element of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathbb {R}}^3\approx \mathfrak {so}^*(3)$$\end{document}R3≈so∗(3), the angular momentum \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J^{SO(3)}$$\end{document}JSO(3) is given by\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \langle J^{SO(3)}(c), Y\rangle = \int (1+\kappa ^2)\langle c\times D_sc, Y\circ c\rangle \textrm{d}s, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}⟨JSO(3)(c),Y⟩=∫(1+κ2)⟨c×Dsc,Y∘c⟩ds,which can be understood as the angular momentum of a thickened curve where the thickness (or mass) at each point is a function of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$1+\kappa ^2$$\end{document}1+κ2. Note, that this is in stark contrast to the previous section, i.e., the length-weighted case, where the angular momentum for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Omega ^{\Phi (\ell )}$$\end{document}ΩΦ(ℓ) is just the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Phi (\ell )$$\end{document}Φ(ℓ)-scaled version of the angular momentum for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Omega ^{\textrm{id}}=3\Omega ^{\operatorname {MW}}$$\end{document}Ωid=3ΩMW.

We have the following result concerning the induced presymplectic structure:

Theorem 5.3

(The presymplectic structure \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Omega ^{1+\kappa ^2}$$\end{document}Ω1+κ2) The induced (pre)symplectic structure of the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G^{1+\kappa ^2}$$\end{document}G1+κ2-metric is given by:9\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned}&\Omega ^{1+\kappa ^2}_c(h,k) =\int 3(1+\kappa ^2) \langle D_s c, h \times k\rangle +(D_s \kappa ^2) \langle c,h \times k\rangle + 4 \kappa ^2\langle D_sh,D_sc\rangle \langle c\times D_s c, k \rangle \\ &\qquad -2\langle D_s^2h, D_s^2c\rangle \langle c\times D_s c, k \rangle -4 \kappa ^2\langle D_sk,D_sc\rangle \langle c\times D_s c,h\rangle + 2\langle D_s^2k, D_s^2c\rangle \langle c\times D_s c,h\rangle \textrm{d}s, \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Ωc1+κ2(h,k)=∫3(1+κ2)⟨Dsc,h×k⟩+(Dsκ2)⟨c,h×k⟩+4κ2⟨Dsh,Dsc⟩⟨c×Dsc,k⟩-2⟨Ds2h,Ds2c⟩⟨c×Dsc,k⟩-4κ2⟨Dsk,Dsc⟩⟨c×Dsc,h⟩+2⟨Ds2k,Ds2c⟩⟨c×Dsc,h⟩ds,and the vertical vectors \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{a.D_s c \mid a\in C^\infty (S^1)\}\subset T_c \textrm{Imm}$$\end{document}{a.Dsc∣a∈C∞(S1)}⊂TcImm is in the kernel.

Proof of Theorem 5.3

To calculate the formula for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Omega ^{1+\kappa ^2}$$\end{document}Ω1+κ2 we first need the variation of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\kappa ^2=\langle D_s^2 c, D_s^2 c\rangle $$\end{document}κ2=⟨Ds2c,Ds2c⟩. Using, that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$D_{c,h}D_s = -\langle D_s h, D_s c\rangle D_s$$\end{document}Dc,hDs=-⟨Dsh,Dsc⟩Ds, cf. the proof of Lemma 2.4, we calculate:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} D_{c,h} (D_s^2 c)&= (D_{c,h}D_s).D_sc + D_s\left( (D_{c,h}D_s)c\right) + D_s^2 h \\ &=-\langle D_sh,D_sc\rangle .D_s^2c - D_s\left( \langle D_sh, D_sc\rangle D_sc\right) + D_s^2h\\&=-\langle D_sh,D_sc\rangle .D_s^2c - \left( D_s\langle D_sh, D_sc\rangle \right) D_sc -\langle D_sh, D_sc\rangle D^2_sc + D_s^2h\\&=-2\langle D_sh,D_sc\rangle .D_s^2c - \left( D_s\langle D_sh, D_sc\rangle \right) D_sc + D_s^2h \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Dc,h(Ds2c)=(Dc,hDs).Dsc+Ds(Dc,hDs)c+Ds2h=-⟨Dsh,Dsc⟩.Ds2c-Ds⟨Dsh,Dsc⟩Dsc+Ds2h=-⟨Dsh,Dsc⟩.Ds2c-Ds⟨Dsh,Dsc⟩Dsc-⟨Dsh,Dsc⟩Ds2c+Ds2h=-2⟨Dsh,Dsc⟩.Ds2c-Ds⟨Dsh,Dsc⟩Dsc+Ds2hThus we obtain10\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} D_{c,h}\kappa ^2 = -4\langle D_sh,D_sc\rangle \kappa ^2 - 0 + 2\langle D_s^2h, D_s^2c\rangle . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Dc,hκ2=-4⟨Dsh,Dsc⟩κ2-0+2⟨Ds2h,Ds2c⟩.Next we note that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Omega ^{1+\kappa ^2}_c(h,k) = \Omega ^{\textrm{id}}_c(h,k)+\Omega ^{\kappa ^2}_c(h,k) \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Ωc1+κ2(h,k)=Ωcid(h,k)+Ωc2(h,k)as the operation \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L_c\mapsto \Theta _c ^L$$\end{document}Lc↦ΘcL is linear in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L_c$$\end{document}Lc. Using (1), we then calculate\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned} \Omega ^{\kappa ^2}_c(h,k)&=\int \langle D_s c, \kappa ^2 h \times k + h\times \kappa ^2 k\rangle -
