That \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Omega $$\end{document}Ω decends to a form on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$B_i(S^1,\mathbb {R}^3)$$\end{document}Bi(S1,R3) follows again from Theorem 2.6; alternatively, we can also see this directly from the above formula: A straightforward calculation shows that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h=a.D_sc$$\end{document}h=a.Dsc is indeed in the kernel of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Omega _c^{1+\kappa ^2}$$\end{document}Ωc1+κ2. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square $$\end{document}□

OpenProblem 5.4

It remains open if the presymplectic structure \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\bar{\Omega }^{1+\kappa ^2}$$\end{document}Ω¯1+κ2 on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$B_i(S^1,\mathbb {R}^3)$$\end{document}Bi(S1,R3) is non-degenerate and thus symplectic. Therefore it remains to show that tangent vectors of the form \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$aD_sc$$\end{document}aDsc are the whole kernel of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Omega _c^{1+\kappa ^2}$$\end{document}Ωc1+κ2. It seems natural to employ a similar strategy as in the previous section for length-weighted metrics, i.e., for given h we test with all k of the form \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k=ac$$\end{document}k=ac for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a\in C^{\infty }(S^1)$$\end{document}a∈C∞(S1). This leads to reducing the degeneracy of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Omega ^{1+\kappa ^2}$$\end{document}Ω1+κ2 to solving the equation \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$P_c(a)=f$$\end{document}Pc(a)=f for any given \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f\in C^{\infty }(S^1)$$\end{document}f∈C∞(S1), where\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} P_c(a)&:= 2 \langle D_s^2c,c\rangle D_s^2a - 4 \langle D_s c, c \rangle \kappa ^2. D_s a + (3+\kappa ^2)a;. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Pc(a):=2⟨Ds2c,c⟩Ds2a-4⟨Dsc,c⟩κ2.Dsa+(3+κ2)a;.The existence of periodic solutions for the above equation is, however, non-trivial. Note, that the coefficient functions are in general degenerate, e.g., \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\langle D_s^2c,c\rangle $$\end{document}⟨Ds2c,c⟩ can vanish somewhere.

OpenProblem 5.5

We may consider a more general version. Suppose \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L_c:h\mapsto f_c. h$$\end{document}Lc:h↦fc.h where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f_c$$\end{document}fc is a positive function for any c and is of form \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f_c(\theta )=\rho (c(\theta ),D_s c(\theta ), D_s^2 c(\theta ),\ldots , D_s^N c(\theta ))$$\end{document}fc(θ)=ρ(c(θ),Dsc(θ),Ds2c(θ),…,DsNc(θ)) with some finite N and a function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\rho :{\mathbb {R}}^{3N}\rightarrow {\mathbb {R}}_{\ge 0}$$\end{document}ρ:R3N→R≥0. We expect that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\bar{\Omega }^L$$\end{document}Ω¯L is symplectic on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$B_i(S^1,{\mathbb {R}}^3)$$\end{document}Bi(S1,R3) if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Theta ^L$$\end{document}ΘL is not scale-invariant, or on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$B_i(S^1,{\mathbb {R}}^3)/\mathcal {F}$$\end{document}Bi(S1,R3)/F with a two-dimensional distribution \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {F}$$\end{document}F if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Theta ^L$$\end{document}ΘL is scale-invariant (cf. Theorem 3.2).

Numerical Illustrations

In this section we numerically illustrate two Hamiltonian flows with respect to the new symplectic structures introduced in this article. For interested readers, we share video footage of the simulations shown in Figs. 1 and 2; see https://youtu.be/nu09IwRK-tY.Fig. 1Hamiltonian flow of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H_{-2}$$\end{document}H-2, the flux of a rotational vector field from Example 4.5 using \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Phi ({\ell (c)})=10\ell (c)^{-2}$$\end{document}Φ(ℓ(c))=10ℓ(c)-2 (top), and the flow only with its binormal component (bottom). The red, green, and blue axes are the x, y, z axes respectively

Hamiltonian flow of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H_{-2}$$\end{document}H-2, the flux of a rotational vector field from Example 4.5 using \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Phi ({\ell (c)})=10\ell (c)^{-2}$$\end{document}Φ(ℓ(c))=10ℓ(c)-2 (top), and the flow only with its binormal component (bottom). The red, green, and blue axes are the x, y, z axes respectively

For the numerical simulations, we discretized each curve as an ordered sequence of points in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathbb {R}}^3$$\end{document}R3. To approximate terms involving spatial derivatives, such as the binormal vector and the curvature, we follow the methods of discrete differential geometry (see Bobenko (2015)). We then compute the time integration of each Hamiltonian vector field using the explicit Runge–Kutta method of fourth-order in time. We want to emphasize that our numerical examples are only for illustrative purposes and we do not guarantee any correctness of (even short-time) behaviors of the curve dynamics.

In our experiments, we use length-weighted presymplectic structures \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Omega ^{\Phi (\ell )}$$\end{document}ΩΦ(ℓ) (and symplectic structures \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\bar{\Omega }^{\Phi (\ell )}$$\end{document}Ω¯Φ(ℓ) for unparametrized curves) as derived in Sect. 4. That is, we use functions of the form \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Phi (\ell )=C \ell ^p$$\end{document}Φ(ℓ)=Cℓp with some \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C>0$$\end{document}C>0 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p\in {\mathbb {R}}$$\end{document}p∈R. Note that C only works as time-scaling and does not change the orbit under the Hamiltonian flow. This is because in the expression of the field \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\operatorname {hgrad}^{\Omega ^{\Phi (\ell )}} H$$\end{document}hgradΦ(ℓ)H, cf. equation (6), the coefficient C appears only in the factor \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\frac{1}{3\Phi (\ell )}=\frac{1}{C \ell ^p}$$\end{document}13Φ(ℓ)=1Cℓp shared by all the terms and the factor \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\frac{\Phi '(\ell )}{3\Phi (\ell )+\Phi '(\ell )\ell }=\frac{p}{(3+p)\ell }$$\end{document}Φ′(ℓ)3Φ(ℓ)+Φ′(ℓ)ℓ=p(3+p)ℓ does not depend on C. We choose C to run each simulation with a reasonable discrete timestep, but it essentially does not affect the dynamics.

We simulate two Hamiltonian flows (Example 4.5 and 4.8) from Sect. 4. These two examples involve only up to second-order spatial derivatives. Simulating other Hamiltonian flows, such as those discussed in Examples 4.6 and 4.7 having third or higher-order derivatives is more challenging as one would have to discretize these higher-order derivatives more carefully.

As for the initial curve, we consider the trefoil11\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} c(\theta )= \left( (2 + \cos (2\theta ))\cos (3\theta ),(2 + \cos (2\theta )) \sin (3\theta ), \sin (4\theta )\right) , \quad \theta \in S^1={\mathbb {R}}/2\pi \mathbb {Z}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}c(θ)=(2+cos(2θ))cos(3θ),(2+cos(2θ))sin(3θ),sin(4θ),θ∈S1=R/2πZ.in both of our examples.

Example 6.1

(Flux of a vector field) We first simulate the Hamiltonian flow for the Hamiltonian that is defined as the flux of a vector field through a Seifert surface whose boundary is the curve c, cf. Example 4.5. We chose the vector field of a rigid body rotation \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V(x)=v\times x$$\end{document}V(x)=v×x with the rotation axis \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v=\frac{1}{\sqrt{3}}(1,1,1) \in {\mathbb {R}}^3$$\end{document}v=13(1,1,1)∈R3. This amounts to the Hamiltonian \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H_{-2}$$\end{document}H-2 in Example 4.5.

The horizontal Hamiltonian field (7) is a weighted sum of the rotation \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\operatorname {hgrad}^{\Omega ^{\operatorname {MW}}}H_{-2}=v\times c$$\end{document}hgradMWH-2=v×c and the binormal field \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\operatorname {hgrad}^{\Omega ^{\operatorname {MW}}}\ell =D_s^2 c\times D_s c$$\end{document}hgradMWℓ=Ds2c×Dsc with time-constant coefficients. Since these two flows are Poisson commutative, we can simulate the flow by evolving the curves under the binormal equation and rotating it at each time, i.e., \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c_t=\operatorname {exp}(t_1 \hat{v}) c_{t_2}^{\operatorname {Binormal}}$$\end{document}ct=exp(t1v^)ct2Binormal where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{v}\in \mathfrak {so}(3)$$\end{document}v^∈so(3) corresponds v and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t_1, t_2$$\end{document}t1,t2 are time t weighted by the coefficients in (7). Figure 1 illustrates our simulation using \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Phi (\ell {(c)})=10\ell {(c)}^{-2}$$\end{document}Φ(ℓ(c))=10ℓ(c)-2. The top row is the flow of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\operatorname {hgrad}^{\Omega ^{\Phi (\ell )}}H_{-2}$$\end{document}hgradΦ(ℓ)H-2, and the bottom row is the flow by only the binormal equation part where the curve moves toward the z-direction while showing a rotational motion around the z-axis.

Fig. 2Hamiltonian flow of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\operatorname {hgrad}^{\Omega ^{\Phi (\ell )}}E$$\end{document}hgradΦ(ℓ)E with different choices of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Phi (\ell )$$\end{document}Φ(ℓ). Example 6.2

(Total squared scale) Our next example is the squared scale functional E (Example 4.8). Here we test three different choices of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Phi (\ell )=C\ell ^{p}$$\end{document}Φ(ℓ)=Cℓp. Note again that we vary C only for computational purposes and this does not change the trajectory. The simulation results are shown in Fig. 2.

We first compute for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Phi (\ell )=\frac{1}{20}$$\end{document}Φ(ℓ)=120, which corresponds to (a constant multiple of) the Marsden–Weinstein flow \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\operatorname {hgrad}^{\Omega ^{\operatorname {MW}}}E$$\end{document}hgradMWE. The curve moves back and forth in the z-direction, but curve points tend to get stuck once they come closer to the origin as both the term \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$-D_s c\times c$$\end{document}-Dsc×c and the term \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\frac{1}{2}|c|^2 D_s c\times D_s c$$\end{document}12|c|2Dsc×Dsc decrease as c goes to zero. As a result these parts form a complex shape around the origin. The next case is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Phi (\ell )=\frac{1}{20}\ell ^{-1/10}$$\end{document}Φ(ℓ)=120ℓ-1/10. This shows a behavior similar to the first case, but points do not get stuck near the origin due to the additional term in (8). While moving back and forth, the curve does not become as entangled as in the previous case and seems to alternately transform between a trefoil and a trivial knot. The last case is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Phi (\ell )=10^{-5}\ell ^{2}$$\end{document}Φ(ℓ)=10-5ℓ2. This shows a very different evolution. Unlike the other test cases, the curve does not globally translate in the z-direction but forms a complex spiral shape while shrinking slowly. In all three cases, the symmetry of the trefoil, i.e., that rotation of 120 degrees around the z-axis does not change the shape, seems to be preserved in time.
